4.55765 4.79041 4.79103C4.55704 5.0244 4.24051 5.15551 3.91046 5.15551C3.58041 5.15551 3.26388 5.0244 3.0305 4.79103C2.79713 4.55765 2.66602 4.24112 2.66602 3.91107C2.66602 3.58102 2.79713 3.26449 3.0305 3.03112C3.26388 2.79774 3.58041 2.66663 3.91046 2.66663ZM2.84379 6.0444H4.97713V13.3333H2.84379V6.0444ZM6.39935 6.0444H8.44379V7.03996H8.47046C8.7549 6.50663 9.44824 5.93774 10.4882 5.93774C12.6482 5.93774 13.0482 7.35996 13.0482 9.20885V13.3333H10.9149V10.0622C10.9149 9.27996 10.8971 8.27552 9.83046 8.27552C8.73713 8.27552 8.56824 9.12885 8.56824 11.5V6.48936H8.70122L8.94512 4.52812H7.07317V3.27901C7.07317 2.71253 7.22476 2.32648 8.00768 2.32648H9V0.577058C8.82732 0.55327 8.235 0.5 7.54585 0.5C6.10683 0.5 5.12195 1.41103 5.12195 3.08478V4.52812H3.5V6.48936H5.12195V11.5H7.07317Z" fill="currentColor"></path></svg></a></div></div></div></div><div class="flex flex-[7] w-full min-w-0"><div class="flex flex-col gap-6 max-md:flex-col w-full min-w-0"><div><p class="mb-6">For the last several months, we’ve been busy talking to security teams and building answers to the AI-accelerated challenges that you’ve told us you’re facing. Among these are the overwhelming volume and velocity of alerts, exacerbated by attackers equipped with AI. <a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/threat-command/ai-agent-attack-detection-hugging-face-breach" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">The Hugging Face incident</a> demonstrated another core challenge in the security operations center (SOC). Even if all of the signals are detected, that’s not enough, unless something connects them as part of a single attack. Combined, these problems force most teams to ration coverage by headcount, so the contents of the alert queue decide what gets attention, and the rest ages out unexamined. Spending time on proactive defenses then feels even more challenging. </p><p class="mb-6">Today, we’re giving you a peek at AlertZero, the new agentic layer of Elastic Security. AlertZero has a straightforward premise: put SOC teams on the path to the equivalent of inbox zero for alerts, with every alert answered. It does this by reducing the existing queue and the false positives that contribute to it, through high-volume correlation and enrichment, proposing actions, and then helping to create and tune detections. </p><p class="mb-6">AlertZero has agents, called Watches, each with one named job: Triage, Hunt, Detection, or Forensics. The Watches run on triggers and schedules to propose evidence-backed conclusions, called Proposed Actions, for approval, modification, escalation, or dismissal. Each Watch surfaces decisions to the analyst based on its autonomy level: manual, assisted, or supervised. Regardless of level, every consequential action is proposed to the analyst for approval.</p><p class="mb-6">AlertZero retains the same ″open by design″ philosophy as Elastic Security. You can use the model that works best for your team, including proprietary and open source models, with the same product and features, across Elastic Cloud, self-managed, or fully air-gapped.</p><p class="mb-6">We didn’t think of this overnight. AlertZero builds on three years of generative AI (GenAI) innovation in Elastic Security. <a href="https://www.elastic.co/blog/introducing-elastic-ai-assistant" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Elastic AI Assistant</a> started with conversational help in 2023. <a href="https://www.elastic.co/blog/whats-new-elastic-security-8-14-0" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Attack Discovery</a> extended that into a dedicated investigation task in 2024, connecting related alerts into an attack narrative. <a href="https://www.elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/ai/agent-builder/agent-builder" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Elastic Agent Builder</a> and <a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/blog/skills-elastic-security-9-4" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">security skills</a> added agents that use tools and apply domain expertise, while <a href="https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/blog/security-automation-with-elastic-workflows" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Elastic Workflows</a> supplies repeatable execution. AlertZero brings these capabilities together, tailored to the responsibilities of a SOC.</p><p class="mb-6"></p><h2 id="alertzero-watches-stay-in-their-lane-(and-they-handle-it-well)" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7"><strong>AlertZero Watches stay in their lane (and they handle it well)</strong></h2><p class="mb-6">Every Watch in AlertZero is directly aligned with the key responsibilities in the SOC, and their levels of autonomy are customizable. Within each Watch, Workers carry out specific tasks. For example, a Triage Worker runs Attack Discovery, which connects related alerts into attack narratives, while a Forensics Worker examines <span role="button" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R1q42lpnl6:" class="glossary-popover-trigger cursor-help border-b border-dashed border-current">endpoint</span> activity. Your team controls how much each Worker can do without human review, so you can tailor Watches to help your team in the places you most need it. </p><p class="mb-6">The upcoming technical preview introduces four Watches:</p><div class="mb-6 overflow-x-auto"><table class="w-full border border-solid border-default"><thead class="bg-surface-subtlest"><tr class="border-b border-solid border-default"><th class="px-4 py-2 text-left text-sm font-bold"><p class="mb-6"><strong>Watch name</strong></p></th><th class="px-4 py-2 text-left text-sm font-bold"><p class="mb-6"><strong>What it helps your team do</strong></p></th></tr></thead><tbody><tr class="border-b border-solid border-default"><td class="px-4 py-2 text-sm"><p class="mb-6"><strong>Triage</strong></p></td><td class="px-4 py-2 text-sm"><p class="mb-6">Assesses alerts, connects related activity, and identifies findings that need attention.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-solid border-default"><td class="px-4 py-2 text-sm"><p class="mb-6"><strong>Hunt</strong></p></td><td class="px-4 py-2 text-sm"><p class="mb-6">Uses threat research to look for evidence of attacks in the available telemetry.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-solid border-default"><td class="px-4 py-2 text-sm"><p class="mb-6"><strong>Detection</strong></p></td><td class="px-4 py-2 text-sm"><p class="mb-6">Investigates noisy rules and coverage gaps and then prepares detection changes for review.</p></td></tr><tr class="border-b border-solid border-default"><td class="px-4 py-2 text-sm"><p class="mb-6"><strong>Forensics</strong></p></td><td class="px-4 py-2 text-sm"><p class="mb-6">Examines endpoint activity to establish what happened and to identify supported response actions.</p></td></tr></tbody></table></div><p class="mb-6"></p><figure class="mb-6 w-full"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rq62lpnl6:"><img alt="AI SOC automation org chart: AlertZero&#x27;s Triage, Hunt, Detection and Forensics Watches with example Workers" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltda2b4fef47f5896e/6ac735659df04bbb1e32c902/image5.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><figcaption class="mt-4 text-center text-sm text-subtler"><p><em>Figure 1. The four Watches align automated security tasks with familiar SOC responsibilities. Analysts choose which tasks to run and how much to delegate.</em></p></figcaption></figure><p class="mb-6">A hunt can start from new threat research, and detection analysis can start from recurring false positives. Endpoint analysis can start from a finding that needs a closer look, and all of the Watches can run on triggers or a schedule. Watches don’t form a mandatory pipeline. </p><h2 id="how-alertzero-handles-suspicious-login-sessions" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7"><strong>How AlertZero handles suspicious login sessions</strong></h2><p class="mb-6">The following example shows how the Triage and Forensics Hunts work together. We’re starting on the <strong>Proposed Actions</strong> page, where Watches awaiting human approval show their work before carrying out an action. </p><figure class="mb-6 w-full"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rq82lpnl6:"><img alt="AlertZero Proposed Actions page grouping AI SOC automation tasks under Respond, Investigate and Configure" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt135d2187bba6926c/6ac73584d0560246cb294eb4/image4.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><figcaption class="mt-4 text-center text-sm text-subtler"><p><em>Figure 2. AlertZero’s list of Proposed Actions, organized by the type of action awaiting human input.</em></p></figcaption></figure><p class="mb-6">Proposed Actions are grouped by the type of action they’re waiting for. In this specific scenario, under <strong>Respond</strong>, an impossible-travel finding for an executive account, cfo@corp, has an endpoint-isolation action waiting for review. Before we even open this screen, the Triage Watch and Forensics Watch have already gathered the findings behind that recommendation. </p><p class="mb-6">Opening the item reveals the associated Investigation and its supporting evidence. </p><figure class="mb-6 w-full"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rq9ilpnl6:"><img alt="AlertZero Investigation showing impossible travel alert triage evidence for a compromised executive account" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt723c95436a33a200/6ac735a2758e4e1fcbe1508a/image2.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><figcaption class="mt-4 text-center text-sm text-subtler"><p><em>Figure 3. The analyst opens the evidence behind the Proposed Action in its associated Investigation.</em></p></figcaption></figure><p class="mb-6">In this example, the account was active in Boston and then, 39 minutes later, it was active from a distant hosting network, using the same session identifier without a fresh multifactor authentication (MFA) event. </p><p class="mb-6">The endpoint findings add more context: an unsigned process accessing browser session material. If needed, we can examine how that process started and what it contacted, along with the time period covered. To see the full scope of the incident, there are the related alerts and affected entities, in addition to endpoint findings, in the same flyout. In this case, the pattern warrants investigating session replay. Virtual private network (VPN) use and proxies also need consideration, as does inaccurate geolocation.</p><p class="mb-6">From the Investigation’s conversation, we can ask these follow-up questions before deciding how to proceed: <em>What did the account access after the suspicious sign-in? What would isolating the endpoint interrupt?</em> These questions also prompt further analysis, and the existing findings are available as context.</p><figure class="mb-6 w-full"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rqbilpnl6:"><img alt="AI incident response flow in AlertZero: open a Proposed Action, review evidence, ask questions, then decide" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6fc27d50a01a889b/6ac735cfd31d47b253b8c2c7/image1.gif" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><figcaption class="mt-4 text-center text-sm text-subtler"><p><em>Figure 4. Review begins with a Proposed Action. The analyst can inspect evidence and ask questions before deciding; approved actions record their execution outcome.</em></p></figcaption></figure><p class="mb-6">In this scenario, the endpoint response is set to manual review. So, after checking the target, rationale, and likely impact, we can approve or decline the Proposed Action. An approval would trigger host isolation through <a href="https://www.elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/endpoint-response-actions/isolate-host" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Elastic Defend</a>. The Investigation records the decision and the execution outcome separately for auditability.</p><p class="mb-6">When an Investigation (or set of Investigations) needs a coordinated and constrained response, an analyst can open an Escalation and link the relevant Investigations. </p><figure class="mb-6 w-full"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rqd2lpnl6:"><img alt="AlertZero escalation dialog linking an investigation and choosing teammates for coordinated response" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt8e7d9a22cac4a3dc/6ac735e1758e4e0830e1508e/image6.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><figcaption class="mt-4 text-center text-sm text-subtler"><p><strong><em>Figure 5. Teammates and an attached Investigation share one Escalation conversation.</em></strong></p></figcaption></figure><p class="mb-6">Teammates can discuss the evidence and, in the same conversation, ask an agent follow-up questions. They can also opt to never invoke an agent in particularly sensitive situations.</p><h2 id="beyond-alert-triage:-what-else-does-alertzero-do?" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7"><strong>Beyond alert triage: What else does AlertZero do?</strong></h2><p class="mb-6">Reducing the alert queue by handling the correlation and enrichment at scale is one part of the solution. On the other side of things, we’re helping with the work that happens when nothing is on fire. </p><h3 id="a-head-start-on-your-hunt" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7"><strong>A head start on your hunt</strong></h3><p class="mb-6">For threat hunting, the Hunt Watch:</p><ul class="mb-6 pl-6 [&amp;&gt;li]:list-disc"><li class="text-subtler"><p class="mb-6">Continuously observes your environment.</p></li><li class="text-subtler"><p class="mb-6">Maps observed technologies and exposures to relevant threats.</p></li><li class="text-subtler"><p class="mb-6">Proposes hunts, findings, coverage improvements, or escalations.</p></li></ul><p class="mb-6">Its core purpose is to answer the question <em>Is there evidence of this threat in my environment?</em>, without requiring you to provoke the hunt or feed the intel. </p><p class="mb-6">The Hunt Watch gets you started with threat research, even when no detection alert has been fired. It relates that research to the environment and available telemetry and searches for relevant indicators. It also examines supporting behavior. The findings identify what was searched and what was found.</p><h3 id="turning-false-positives-and-coverage-gaps-into-rule-changes" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-3xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7"><strong>Turning false positives and coverage gaps into rule changes</strong></h3><p class="mb-6">To improve your environment-specific defenses, hunt findings can inform the Detection Watch by looking at whether existing rules would detect the behavior.</p><p class="mb-6">Suppose a legitimate administrative tool repeatedly triggers a rule. The closure itself is useful feedback, but someone still has to connect several decisions to a common cause in the rule. This is how false-positive closures provide another input for the Detection Watch to contextualize. The Watch reviews the alert examples and existing logic and then prepares a proposed change, like an exception, with the supporting diagnosis. From there, a detection engineer can assess whether a narrow exception fits the benign activity while preserving detection of malicious use of the same tool.</p><p class="mb-6">As an example, the Watch would run an <a href="https://www.elastic.co/docs/explore-analyze/query-filter/languages/esql" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">Elasticsearch Query Language (ES|QL)</a> <span role="button" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:R3mj2lpnl6:" class="glossary-popover-trigger cursor-help border-b border-dashed border-current">query</span> like this:</p><div class="relative mb-6 w-full max-w-full overflow-hidden"><pre class="w-full max-w-full overflow-x-auto p-4"><code class="font-mono text-sm whitespace-pre">FROM .alerts-security.alerts-*
| WHERE @timestamp &gt;= NOW() - 7 days
    AND kibana.alert.workflow_status == &quot;closed&quot;
    AND kibana.alert.workflow_reason == &quot;false_positive&quot;
| STATS false_positives = COUNT(*)
    BY kibana.alert.rule.uuid, kibana.alert.rule.name
| SORT false_positives DESC
| LIMIT 20</code></pre><button title="Copy code block" class="absolute top-1 right-1 z-10 cursor-pointer bg-surface-subtlest p-1"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" fill="none" viewBox="0 0 24 24" stroke-width="1.5" stroke="currentColor" class="size-5"><path stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" d="M15.75 17.25v3.375c0 .621-.504 1.125-1.125 1.125h-9.75a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125V7.875c0-.621.504-1.125 1.125-1.125H6.75a9.06 9.06 0 0 1 1.5.124m7.5 10.376h3.375c.621 0  1.125-.504 1.125-1.125V11.25c0-4.46-3.243-8.161-7.5-8.876a9.06 9.06 0 0 0-1.5-.124H9.375c-.621 0-1.125.504-1.125  1.125v3.5m7.5 10.375H9.375a1.125 1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-9.25m12 6.625v-1.875a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375h-1.5a1.125  1.125 0 0 1-1.125-1.125v-1.5a3.375 3.375 0 0 0-3.375-3.375H9.75"></path></svg></button></div><p class="mb-6">Once the Watch reviews the rules’ rankings based on their recently closed false positives, it identifies those with high counts to investigate further. A high count doesn’t necessarily prove that the rule is defective, but the analysis needs to inspect the examples and their closure rationale, because an incorrect false-positive label can otherwise become an incorrect exclusion.</p><p class="mb-6">In this example, the Detection Watch’s Rule Tuning Worker is set to manual. After the analyst authorizes the analysis, the resulting Proposed Action brings together the diagnosis, the motivating alerts, the existing logic, the proposed logic, and backtest results. That gives a detection engineer an actual change to assess and the evidence used to construct it.</p><figure class="mb-6 w-full"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rql2lpnl6:"><img alt="AlertZero rule tuning proposal to reduce false positives, with current query, proposed query and backtest" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt19a25549fbfc8dd5/6ac73611e8ffc64c3fb1792e/image3.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><figcaption class="mt-4 text-center text-sm text-subtler"><p><em>Figure 6. A rule-tuning Proposed Action includes examples, current and proposed logic, and a backtest. Five sample alerts become zero in this example; that result alone doesn’t establish preserved attack coverage.</em></p></figcaption></figure><p class="mb-6">With the proposal, we can see that the five sample false positives no longer match the revised query.</p><p class="mb-6">We can open the Proposed Action to review the change and ask follow-up questions in the linked Investigation, such as <em>What other activity would this exclusion suppress?</em> Because this backtest only covers benign samples, the analyst still needs to validate detection of known malicious activity through the team’s existing testing process before approving. If it’s approved, the change is applied;  if it’s declined, the rule is unchanged.</p><p class="mb-6"></p><h2 id="you-can-decide-how-much-to-delegate" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7"><strong>You can decide how much to delegate</strong></h2><p class="mb-6">You choose which Watches run and how far each goes, with autonomy configurable per Worker. We’ve built this into AlertZero because no two security teams are organized the same way. Some run tiers, some run pods, many have no detection engineer, and most cannot staff nights. Regardless of team structure, autonomy also is configurable because different jobs carry different risks, like assessing an alert versus responding on an endpoint. You can delegate routine analysis while choosing more cautiously when a host may be affected. As an example, the Triage Watch Worker runs Attack Discovery, while a Forensics Watch Worker performs endpoint analysis. Each runs as an Elastic Workflow, calling Agent Builder agents and skills for analysis.</p><p class="mb-6">For true-positive or inconclusive Attack Discovery assessments, manual review lets the analyst decide whether to close an Investigation or request endpoint analysis. In supervised operation, a true-positive assessment can continue into endpoint analysis automatically. An inconclusive assessment still needs a person&#x27;s decision.</p><p class="mb-6">Endpoint analysis has its own control. Manual operation presents supported response actions for review. Supervised operation is designed to permit host isolation, process termination, and process suspension without an additional approval for each action. Detection changes continue to require approval. Teams can therefore automate one part of the sequence while retaining review of another.</p><figure class="mb-6 w-full"><button type="button" class="inline-block border-0 bg-transparent p-0 text-inherit focus-visible:outline-none" tabindex="0" data-base-ui-click-trigger="" id="base-ui-:Rqp2lpnl6:"><img alt="AlertZero Forensics Watch autonomy settings: manual or supervised AI incident response on endpoints" src="https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt06e146584f4dd739/6ac736331cd746ea8f17d9b7/image7.png" class="block h-auto max-w-full cursor-zoom-in focus-visible:outline-none focus-visible:ring-2 focus-visible:ring-current focus-visible:ring-offset-2 w-full"/></button><figcaption class="mt-4 text-center text-sm text-subtler"><p><em>Figure 7. Endpoint analysis has its own autonomy control; manual review is selected here.</em></p></figcaption></figure><h2 id="every-alert-is-answered,-and-every-decision-is-yours" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7"><strong>Every alert is answered, and every decision is yours</strong></h2><p class="mb-6">As AI that helps move an investigation forward, AlertZero connects work that too often stops at a handoff. The evidence gathered during triage can guide endpoint analysis and support a response decision. It can also give teammates a place to continue the investigation. Teams choose what to delegate and where their judgment is needed.</p><p class="mb-6">To know when future AlertZero news is published, <a href="https://www.elastic.co/security/alertzero-tp-form" class="font-medium underline underline-offset-3">sign up for updates</a>. AlertZero will be available soon to all users of Elastic Security.</p><h2 id="glossary" class="mb-4 scroll-mt-[120px] text-4xl [&amp;:not(:first-child)]:mt-7"><strong>Glossary</strong></h2><p class="mb-6"><strong>AlertZero:</strong> The agentic layer of Elastic Security, connecting alert triage, threat hunting, detection engineering, and endpoint forensics.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Watch:</strong> A grouping of automated tasks around a security responsibility. The initial four are Triage, Hunt, Detection, and Forensics.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Worker:</strong> A defined task implemented as a workflow in Elastic Workflows, using agents and skills to produce findings, evidence, and supported actions.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Proposed Actions:</strong> Recommended changes with supporting evidence and rationale, presented for approval where review is required.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Investigation:</strong> The shared record for a line of inquiry, bringing together findings, affected entities, evidence, and Proposed Actions.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Escalation:</strong> A conversation that a person creates to coordinate work around linked Investigations. Teammates can discuss evidence and ask agents questions; visibility can be public or private.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Autonomy setting:</strong> A per-Worker control over how much work can proceed without review. Its behavior depends on the task.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Consequential action:</strong> An operation that changes the environment, such as isolating a host or editing a rule.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Elastic Defend:</strong> Elastic&#x27;s endpoint security capability, providing supported endpoint response actions.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Attack Discovery:</strong> A capability that connects related alerts into suspected attacks. The Triage Watch uses it to open an Investigation for each identified attack.</p><p class="mb-6"><strong>Elastic Agent Builder, Elastic Workflows:</strong> Extensible building blocks for Watches: Agent Builder provides agents, tools, and skills; Elastic Workflows coordinates execution.</p></div><div class="flex flex-col gap-4 border border-solid border-strongest bg-surface p-6"><p class="md:ml-4"><div class="mb-3 flex flex-wrap items-center gap-2"><a href="/security-labs/blog/category/ai-automation" class="inline-flex items-center gap-1 px-2.5 py-1 rounded-pill border We’ve built this into AlertZero because no two security teams are organized the same way. Some run tiers, some run pods, many have no detection engineer, and most cannot staff nights. Regardless of team structure, autonomy also is configurable because different jobs carry different risks, like assessing an alert versus responding on an endpoint. \u0026 Automation","title_l10n":"AI \u0026 Automation","url":"/security-labs/blog/category/ai-automation","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:18.389Z","updated_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:18.389Z","ACL":{},"_version":1,"coding_language":false,"tags":[],"_in_progress":false,"publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-25T07:28:24.043Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"category_cta":{"category":[],"position":null},"created_at":"2026-08-26T07:19:14.945Z","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","date":"2026-08-25","main_content":{"markdown_l10n":"AI verdict correctness in our security operations center (SOC) is 92%, up from 60%, but we didn't switch models to get there. What we changed is the context the agents get before they decide anything, including the detection rule's investigation guide and user risk data from Workday, along with the closure reasons from 30 days of past cases on that same rule.\n\nThis post covers how the agentic SOC pipeline is built in Elastic Workflows and Elastic Agent Builder, down to the prompts and the feedback loop that lets an agent see where it got the same rule wrong last time.\n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blte42f05563385cc8c/6a8e92adf5fdbd3289835dd8/image2.png)\n\n\n## Customer Zero: Running Agent Builder in our own SOC\n\nAt Elastic, our internal SOC operates as Customer Zero, meaning that we’re the first and most demanding user of every feature we ship. We run the newest versions of [Elastic Security](https://www.elastic.co/guide/en/security/current/) and [Agent Builder](https://www.elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/ai/agent-builder/agent-builder) in our production environment, often before they reach general availability (GA), across a globally distributed fleet of laptops, servers, and cloud workloads. The workflows and agent configurations shown in this post reflect our setup as of version 9.5.1.\n\n## When your AI SOC analyst is wrong 40% of the time\n\nOur team dove in headfirst with AI agents and fully integrated our alerts with AI triage. When our agents were looking at only the current alert context and investigation indexes, they weren’t always correct. Actually, our logs showed accuracy hovering around 60%. It’s great to have this data, but not if the analysts can’t trust it.\n\nWe were adding long AI summaries to each case, what we would consider *AI slop*, as it was inaccurate 40% of the time. The feedback we got from the analysts was that they weren’t reading them. The analysts started ignoring the AI summaries completely since they couldn't trust that they were helpful or accurate. It took more time to read a paragraph of incorrect information than to just triage the case manually. The summaries were slowing analysts down without providing any benefit worth the additional token cost.\n\n### Leading with the data\n\nBefore getting too in the weeds, here’s the data. Our AI verdict correctness (based on comparing the AI verdict and the analyst close reason) went from 60% to 92% after implementing the changes we discuss in this blog. We’re tracking these metrics using [Elastic dashboards](https://www.elastic.co/docs/explore-analyze/dashboards/managing) by comparing the case custom fields that are discussed more below. This increase in accuracy meant that the analysts could start double-checking the summary and closing the case right away. This changed our AI summaries from being a time sink to allowing our analysts to close the case in one step.  \n\n![Elastic dashboard showing AI verdict accuracy rising from 60% to 92% after the Brainstorm agents launched.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltfacc5caae0537201/6a8e92b1125ece5c2075eca7/image5.png \"Elastic dashboard showing AI verdict accuracy rising from 60% to 92% after the Brainstorm agents launched.\")\n\n### What context AI alert triage actually needs\n\nWe significantly increased agent accuracy by feeding them more context. Here's what we pull in from each source before an agent makes a verdict:  \n\n![Context enrichment sources for AI alert triage: Kibana API and ES|QL lookups with index and endpoint detail.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt0b62927c2c74c2f1/6a8e92b4f65645961154b6fc/image9.png \"Context enrichment sources for AI alert triage: Kibana API and ES|QL lookups with index and endpoint detail.\")\n\n### When should you use an AI agent instead of a query?\n\nIt's important to know when to use AI and when not to. If the answer requires a predictable query with only a variable or two changing each time, don't use an agent. Instead, use an [Elastic workflow](https://www.elastic.co/docs/explore-analyze/workflows) that runs an Elasticsearch Query Language (ES|QL) query, a Kibana API call, or a GET request. They're faster and cheaper, and we keep them modular and reusable across many different orchestrators, so a UserDetailsLookup or PastCasesByRulenameLookup can be called from any workflow that needs it. Agents are more suited for tasks that require reading and reasoning that cannot be completed with a simple query; for example, analyzing past case comments for patterns.\n\nWe named our workflows to reflect the three types of activities in the main orchestrator:\n\n1. **Lookup:** ES|QL queries, Kibana API calls, and GET requests to external services.  \n2. **Agent:** AI agents built in [Kibana's Agent Builder](https://www.elastic.co/docs/solutions/security/ai/agent-builder/agent-builder).  \n3. **Action:** POST requests to Kibana case comments and to Slack and other external services.\n\n### Investigation guide lookup: Per-rule triage instructions\n\nUsing the `kibana.alert.rule.uuid`, we can do a GET request to `/api/alerting/rule/\\\u003ckibana.alert.rule.uuid\\\u003e` to grab the investigation guide attached to the detection rule that triggered the alert. Here, we can provide context to *both* the analyst and our AI agents. Our detection engineers fill out these investigation guides when creating the detection, sometimes separating the analyst sections and AI sections with notes to follow. This gives us specific, per-rule instructions so the AI triages each alert type the same way every time. Here's where to find the investigation guide on a rule:  \n\n![Navigating to the alert’s associated investigation guide.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blta2f2572f70f0e806/6a8e92b7cf1e0e9ada75653b/image14.png \"Navigating to the alert’s associated investigation guide.\")\n\n### The user details lookup: Finding high-risk users\n\nWe look up user details in Workday using the `user.email` field from the alert. This gives us context on high-risk users, for example:\n\n- Users who have joined the company in the last 90 days.  \n- Users who are leaving the company in the next 90 days.  \n- Cost centers that carry elevated risk (for example, help desk, security researchers).\n\n![Kibana workflow on UserDetailsLookup.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt32c052dc55609d9e/6a8e92bab1ddcc0bf68bff3a/image10.png \"Kibana workflow on UserDetailsLookup.\")\n\n### Past cases by rule name: What verdict did the analyst choose?\n\nWe look up alerts with the same `kibana.alert.rule.uuid` from the last 30 days that have been tied to cases and closed. This gives our agents a historical baseline. If this rule has fired 50 times and 48 were false positives, that's critical context. An agent that knows the history of a rule makes significantly better verdicts than one that doesn't.\n\n![Kibana workflow on PastCasesByRulenameLookup.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt182755d1e997bf35/6a8e92bdf61d6e5ef29d4db4/image7.png \"Kibana workflow on PastCasesByRulenameLookup.\")\n\nUsing the cases we've seen in the last 30 days, we pull the fields that tell us how analysts actually closed them, the verdict they chose, their summary of the activity, and whether the AI got it right. These are the signals that close the loop:  \n\n![Analyst-selected close reason and custom fields displayed on Kibana Cases.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt89bda7e391cd877c/6a8e92bf3e4fd503671a8831/side.png \"Analyst-selected close reason and custom fields displayed on Kibana Cases.\")\n\n## The AI feedback loop: How agents learn from wrong verdicts\n\nUsing the data returned from the PastCasesbyRulenameLookup, if the analyst selects **False Positive** but the AI verdict was **True Positive \\- Suspicious** in previous cases, our agents can reference the analyst closure notes and case comments to understand why the previous verdict was incorrect. This context is fed to the agent before it makes a decision on the new case.  \n\n![Case fields table showing which alert triage fields are filled by the analyst and which by the AI agent.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt5f43349a4e7c2061/6a8e92c23e4fd5263b1a8835/image1.png \"Case fields table showing which alert triage fields are filled by the analyst and which by the AI agent.\")\n\nThe **AI Incorrect?** custom field toggle is optional. An analyst can select it and fill out why they feel the AI verdict was wrong in the 'Detail on AI Incorrectness' custom field. If the AI verdict itself was correct but the “AI Generated Summary” case comment went in the wrong direction, the analyst can also explain here where the comment was incorrect. Even without this field, we can determine AI verdict correctness because the two AI agent–generated fields (AI Confidence and AI Verdict) are always on the alert via the workflows shown here, and the analyst is required to pick a closure reason. Our automation team pulls these custom fields from Kibana cases into a dashboard so we can track incorrect verdicts over time by comparing the analyst closure reason and AI verdict to tune our agent prompts or provide additional context where needed.\n\nHere's the feedback loop in action, where our Pattern Finder Agent catches its own overclassification pattern in previous cases and surfaces tuning requests already filed against this rule:\n\n![Real example of mistake auto-correction under AI Generated Summary case comment.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt883e9bd5804b1e3e/6a8e92c5d98b5f9062b152b4/image13.png \"Real example of mistake auto-correction under AI Generated Summary case comment.\")\n\n## How the agentic SOC pipeline runs in Elastic Workflows\n\nWe have one overarching Elastic workflow, Agent Brainstorm, that orchestrates 12 child workflows (and growing). The name reflects what's happening inside: one parent workflow (Agent Brainstorm), many context lookups, and three agents working together to reach a combined AI-generated case summary.\n\n![Kibana Agent Builder setup with three agents.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt6de921de94d80580/6a8e92c8125ece7dda75ecab/image11.png \"Kibana Agent Builder setup with three agents.\")\n\n| Agent | Tools assigned | Inputs | Output | External queries |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| Pattern Finder | None | User details, investigation guide, past cases, current event | JSON | No |\n| L1 Investigation | Yes | Pattern JSON, investigation guide, current alert | Structured report | Yes |\n| Summarizer | None | Investigator report, pattern JSON, investigation guide, ES\\|QL queries | Markdown for Slack and Cases | No |\n\n### Pattern agent: Finding patterns across closed cases\n\nOur Pattern Finder Agent takes in many inputs (user details, investigation guide, past case records, details about the current event) and makes pattern comparisons across historical cases. *This agent has no tools assigned to it in Agent Builder and doesn’t make any external queries. (It only uses the data given to it.)* That makes this agent much faster and ensures that the behavior cannot be overridden by injected content. Here’s a snippet of the prompt where we talk about our feedback loop:\n\n```\n    ## AI Notes on Patterns\n        Look for patterns across these fields in past_case_records:\n        - workflow_reason (benign_positive, true_positive, etc.) This is the ANALYST PICKED result and should be seen as the truth.\n        - custom_field_ai_verdict. This is picked by AI - Is AI usually correct on this type of case?\n        - custom_field_ai_confidence. This is picked by AI - Is AI usually correct on this type of case?\n        - custom_field_ai_incorrect_analyst_bool - This is a boolean the analyst can choose to say that AI was incorrect here.\n        - custom_field_summary - This is written by an analyst on why they closed the case. This should be seen as the truth.\n        - comments_text (analyst comments and AI summaries)\n        - user_id, user_name, user_email (repeat offenders?)\n        - source_ip, host_name (recurring infrastructure?)\n        - If there is another case with the exact same type of activity, ALWAYS add the case # and a link to the case so we can reference it.\n        - Also consider inputs.user_job_family and inputs.user_job_title when assessing whether the alert pattern makes sense for this user type.\n```\n\nAt the end of the prompt, we have it return its findings in JSON format:\n\n```\n ## Required Output Format\n        Respond with ONLY a valid JSON object, no markdown, no explanation:\n        {\n          \"percentage_false_positive_or_benign_last_50_cases\": \"\u003cX\u003e%\",\n          \"number_of_total_alerts\": \u003cinputs.total_alerts_for_rule\u003e,\n          \"number_of_alerts_worked_in_cases\": \u003cinputs.total_closed_with_cases\u003e,\n          \"uniqueness\": \"RARE or COMMON\",\n          \"high_risk_employee\": \"NEW HIRE or LEAVING SOON or NO\",\n          \"job_title\": \"\u003cinputs.user_job_title or unknown\u003e\",\n          \"cost_center\": \"\u003cfrom user details or unknown\u003e\",\n          \"ai_notes_on_patterns\": \"\u003cyour pattern analysis here\u003e\",\n          \"ai_pattern_TLDR\":\"\u003cshortened_version_if_notes_long\u003e\",\n          \"recommendation_for_severity\": \"medium or high or critical\"\n          \"explanation_on_severity_change_or_no\": \"\u003cexplanation_here\u003e\"\n        }\n```\n\n### L1 Investigation Agent: Gathering evidence and reaching a verdict\n\nThe Investigation Agent takes in the JSON from the Pattern Finder Agent, the investigation guide, and information about the current case and alert. Most importantly, it first looks at the investigation guide we grabbed above, which our detection engineers fill out with per-detection rule instructions that are the most correct for the agent to follow. If there’s no investigation guide, the agent falls back to its generic alert triage instructions:\n\n```\n# Triage Protocol\n\u003e **📋 Investigation Priority:** If an Investigation Guide is provided in the input, follow its triage steps as your primary instructions. The steps below are **fallback guidance only** — use them when no Investigation Guide is available or to fill gaps the guide does not cover.\n```\n\nUsing the investigation guide and its own judgment, this agent makes external queries to gather additional evidence and reach a verdict on the case. At the end of the prompt, we have the investigator agent return a report:\n\n```\nINVESTIGATOR_REPORT_START\n\n\u003cemoji\u003e Verdict: \u003cverdict\u003e | 🎯 Confidence: \u003c0–100%\u003e | 📋 Reason: \u003cmax 50 words\u003e |\n\nINPUT_TYPE: Alert or Case\nRULE_NAME_OR_CASE_TITLE: \u003c\u003e\nSEVERITY: \u003c\u003e\nSTATUS: \u003c\u003e\nTIMESTAMP: \u003c\u003e\nALERT_QUERY: \u003craw detection query from kibana.alert.rule.parameters — omit entire field if not retrievable\u003e\nWHY_FIRED: \u003c1 sentence — specific technical condition that matched\u003e\nWHEN_MALICIOUS: \u003c1 sentence — malicious pattern and whether this alert DOES or DOES NOT match it\u003e\n\nACTOR: \u003cuser.name\u003e | ASSET_INFO: \u003c\u003e\nHOST: \u003chost.name\u003e | ASSET_INFO: \u003c\u003e\nKEY_ACTION: \u003cevent.action\u003e via \u003cprocess.name\u003e\nCOMMAND_CONTEXT: \u003cprocess.command_line or case description snippet\u003e\nOBSERVABLES: \u003clist key IPs, hashes, users, projects\u003e\n\nEXECUTIVE_SUMMARY: \u003c2–3 sentence summary\u003e\n\nINVESTIGATION_FINDINGS: \u003cdetailed findings from queries, enrichment, pivoting\u003e\n\nCORRELATED_ALERT_CLUSTER: \u003cIf the concurrent pivot returned results, render the full table here with columns: Time | Rule | Severity | Detail. Include the time window\n(e.g. \"±5 min around 18:16 UTC\"). If the pivot returned 0 results, write\n\"No correlated alerts found in ±5 min window.\"\u003e\n\nPATTERN_FINDER_ALIGNMENT: \u003cdoes the current alert match or contradict the PatternFinder analysis? explain\u003e\n\nESQL_QUERIES_RUN: \u003ccomma-separated list of raw ES|QL query strings run during investigation\u003e\n\nRECOMMENDED_ACTIONS:\n- CONFIRM_ACTIVITY: \u003cwhat to look at or who to ask\u003e\n- REMEDIATION: \u003ccontainment, tuning, or additional data needed\u003e\n- RULE_TUNING: \u003cspecific improvement or \"Rule performed as intended — no tuning needed\"\u003e\n\nINVESTIGATOR_REPORT_END\n```\n\n### Summarizer Agent: Writing the case comment that analysts read\n\nThe Summarizer Agent is responsible for formatting the final AI summary that analysts see in the case comment. Like the Pattern Finder Agent, it has no tools assigned to it in Agent Builder and works only from data passed to it. This keeps it fast. Each time a new alert comes in for the same case, we rerun the Agent Brainstorm workflow and replace the case comment only if it provides a new verdict or new important evidence; otherwise, we skip it. We allow the summarizer to keep replacing itself up until the case is assigned to an analyst, at which point we stop.\n\nHere’s a snippet of the summarizer prompt:\n\n```\n# Role \u0026 Objective\nYou are an expert InfoSec report formatter at Elastic. You receive a structured investigation report from the Investigator agent and a pattern analysis from the PatternFinder agent. Your sole job is to combine these into a high-fidelity Markdown summary formatted for Slack. You run NO queries and make NO investigative decisions — all findings and verdicts come from the Investigator and PatternFinder.\n\nYou will also receive:\n- `INVESTIGATOR_REPORT` — the full structured output from the Investigator agent\n- `PATTERN_FINDER_OUTPUT` — the JSON analysis from the PatternFinder agent\n- `INVESTIGATION_GUIDE` - the investigation guide attached to the alert that gives suggestions to analyst and AI on how to handle this case\n- `ESQL_QUERIES` — comma-separated ES|QL query strings run during investigation, to be formatted as reference links\n```\n\nThen we ask it to build an output template to post to the case comment:\n\n```\n# Final Output Template\n\n🤖 **AI Generated Summary** 🤖\n\n`\u003cemoji\u003e Verdict: \u003cverdict\u003e | 🎯 Confidence: \u003c0–100%\u003e | 📋 Reason: \u003cmax 50 words\u003e |`\n\n**⚡ TLDR**\n\u003e {1-2 sentences max. Who did what, on what system, and what the verdict is. Written for an analyst who has 5 seconds. No jargon, no hedging.}\n\n** Investigation Guide Suggestions** (only include if it is present)\n- Quick summary of what the analyst should do next based on the investigation guide or how to confirm this activity. Use bullet points if possible\n\n....shortened for purposes of this blog...\n```\n\nNow that we’ve reviewed each agent's role, here's how they're configured in workflow YAML. Each agent is executed one after the other in a synchronous way, waiting for the one above it to finish:\n\n![YAML setup of our three agents in the Agent Brainstorm workflow.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltdeb7091f4a0e7714/6a8e92cb13070e739f2046ee/image8.png \"YAML setup of our three agents in the Agent Brainstorm workflow.\")\n\nWe use two workflows to make POST requests at the end of the Agent Brainstorm to attach the generated final AI summary to both the Kibana case and Slack. Below are some real examples from our environment:\n\n![Comment on Kibana case](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blted31c13539b08296/6a8e92cef61d6e3f039d4db8/image4.png \"Comment on Kibana case\")\n\nWe send alert details to our team Slack channel so that we can monitor the alerts from our phone. Our Slack bot in this channel allows us to perform activities, like **Acknowledge Case** or **Merge Case**, all from Slack, allowing us to quickly take action, even directly from a mobile device. After implementing the work in this blog, we added the **Close Case: AI Correct** button, which you can click to trigger a workflow that assigns you to the case, fills out the closure notes, tags the case, and closes it.   \n\n![Slack message with AI Verdict added.](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/blt17da4516763b2247/6a8e92d11106e83df99c4ad6/image6.png \"Slack message with AI Verdict added.\")\n\nOur AI triager that started off as AI slop is now a core part of how our SOC operates. We’ve kept our analysts in the loop and enabled them to close cases in seconds with our one-button close after AI triages the case. If you're building something similar, we'd love to hear about it in the [Elastic Security community forum](https://discuss.elastic.co/c/security).\n\n## Frequently asked questions\n\n**Why three agents instead of one agent with a lot of skills?**  \nA member of our team did a [cost analysis](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/agentic-soc-token-budget-architecture) of using one agent with a lot of skills versus multiple agents and the token usage came back significantly lower with the latter. \n\n**Why don’t we just have the agent run the query rather than providing it with data we’ve already received from ES|QL?**  \nAI agents process pre-fetched data significantly faster than querying indexes directly, and it's cheaper.\n\n## What to build after L1 triage automation\n\nAfter the L1 agent runs, a great next step is to dispatch specialized agents based on the alert type or data source; for example, a Windows forensics agent or an Azure agent, or possibly a Linux forensics agent. Read our team's writeup on [L2 and L3 agent escalation](https://www.elastic.co/security-labs/alert-triage-agentic-soc-elastic-workflows).","body":{"content_l10n":{"type":"doc","attrs":{},"uid":"4e51da23610143c88d646ce13ba36f47","children":[{"type":"p","attrs":{},"uid":"8bf64f2ebaf548799760d0fe29089cb2","children":[{"text":""}]}],"_version":4}}},"notebook":"","related_posts":[],"schema_markup":{"faq":{"steps":[]},"how_to":{"description_l10n":"","name_l10n":"","steps":[]},"position":false},"seo":{"seo_title_l10n":"Agentic SOC alert triage: 60% to 92% AI accuracy","seo_description_l10n":"Inside the agentic SOC Elastic's InfoSec team runs in production: the three-agent pipeline, the analyst feedback loop, and the one-button close in Slack.","do_not_index":false,"nofollow":false,"canonical_tag":"","seo_image":null},"summary_l10n":"Elastic's InfoSec team runs three agents that read the detection rule's investigation guide and the closure reasons on 30 days of past cases. Principal Security Assurance Analyst","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T13:05:37.867Z","updated_at":"2026-08-12T13:25:21.798Z","ACL":{},"_version":3,"tags":[],"company_l10n":"","email_id":"","facebook_id":"","image":null,"key":"wieger-van-der-meulen","linkedin_id":"","paragraph_l10n":"","subtitle_l10n":"","twitter_id":"","publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-25T07:28:24.066Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"blog_sources":[{"uid":"blt061006380dd59b43","_content_type_uid":"blog_sources"}],"categories":[{"_content_type_uid":"labs_category","uid":"bltf6bc18cac108f99d","title":"AI \u0026 Automation","title_l10n":"AI \u0026 Automation","url":"/security-labs/blog/category/ai-automation","locale":"en-us","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","updated_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","created_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:18.389Z","updated_at":"2026-08-12T12:28:18.389Z","ACL":{},"_version":1,"coding_language":false,"tags":[],"_in_progress":false,"publish_details":{"time":"2026-08-25T07:28:24.043Z","user":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","environment":"blt835e2c3b7e9eb7fb","locale":"en-us"}}],"created_at":"2026-08-19T14:17:42.587Z","created_by":"blt03b12f5a6591ef8c","date":"2026-08-11T00:00:00.000Z","main_content":{"markdown_l10n":"We gave hundreds of developers an AI agent that can run shell commands, edit files, and call [Model Context Protocol (MCP)](https://modelcontextprotocol.io) servers on their laptops, then realized we had no record of what it actually did. So we built one. One 280-line dependency-free bash script, fired by Cursor's hooks, records every tool call as JSONL, and the [Elastic Agent](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/fleet) already on each endpoint ships it to Elasticsearch. Since the May rollout we have logged over 13 million tool-call events from more than 1,100 machines. A question like \"which hosts ran an agent that read a .pem file last week?\" is one [ES|QL](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/query-languages/esql) query. The worked example here is Cursor end to end, but the pattern works with any agent that offers lifecycle hooks.\n\n![](https://images.contentstack.io/v3/assets/bltefdd0b53724fa2ce/bltd4019dba27166ecb/6a85bb00078290658f3213c5/diagram.png)\n\nBy the end of this post, you will have:\n\n- A hook script that logs every Cursor tool call, from both the IDE and the CLI.  \n- The hook configuration, including the two deployment gotchas that cost us the most time.  \n- A way to deliver the collector to machines your device management cannot reach.  \n- an Elastic Agent filestream integration that parses the logs into structured fields  \n- ES|QL queries you can run to hunt across agent activity.  \n- The hardening and privacy decisions we made before rolling this out to the whole company.\n\nEverything here works on the current 9.x release of the Elastic Stack.\n\n## Why AI coding agent activity is a blind spot\n\nA coding agent with shell access is an automated operator on the endpoint. It runs `curl`, installs packages, edits configuration, and reads whatever files its task seems to require. From the point of view of endpoint detection and response (EDR) tooling, this is indistinguishable from the developer doing the same things, because it happens under the developer's account inside processes the developer launched.\n\nThat ambiguity matters in three situations: \n\n1. During an incident, you need to know whether a command was typed by a person or generated by a model that may have been steered by a poisoned README or a malicious MCP server (think of [T1059, Command and Scripting Interpreter](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/), with the model as the interpreter).  \n2. uring threat hunting, you want to ask \"which machines ran an agent that read a file matching `*.pem` last week?\" and get an answer.  \n3. For governance, you need an inventory of which MCP servers your engineers actually connect to, because each one is a third party with tool-level access to a developer conversation.\n\nThe industry has converged on inventory as half of this problem: several major EDR and XDR vendors now ship Shadow AI discovery to find AI tools on endpoints. Inventory shows which machines have Cursor installed. Hooks record what Cursor does once it runs.\n\n## What are agent hooks?\n\nCursor can invoke an external program at defined points in the agent loop: when a session starts, before a shell command runs, after a file edit, when an MCP tool is called, when a sub-agent spawns. The agent writes a JSON payload describing the event to the program's stdin. For some events, the program's stdout response decides whether the action proceeds. Cursor is not alone in offering hooks like these: Claude Code exposes an equivalent set, and we will walk that side in a follow-up post. Here we stay on Cursor.\n\nThe stdout response property means hooks can be a control point. We deliberately chose to use them as a sensor instead. Our script approves everything and records everything, which is the same trade a flight recorder makes: it never flies the plane, but after something goes wrong it is the only honest witness. A blocking hook is one where Cursor pauses the action and waits for the hook's stdout response before proceeding: the agent won't run the shell command / call the MCP tool / read the file / spawn the sub-agent until the hook answers allow, deny, or ask. Blocking was tempting, and we may add targeted controls later, but for a first deployment the goal was visibility without breaking anyone's workflow. An agent rollout that slows developers down gets uninstalled.\n\nThe events we capture are below:\n\n| Hook | Fires when |\n| :---- | :---- |\n| `sessionStart` / `sessionEnd` | A conversation begins or ends |\n| `beforeShellExecution` / `afterShellExecution` | A shell command runs |\n| `beforeMCPExecution` / `afterMCPExecution` | An MCP tool is called |\n| `postToolUse` / `postToolUseFailure` | Any tool call succeeds or fails |\n| `afterFileEdit` / `beforeReadFile` | The agent edits or reads a file |\n| `subagentStart` / `subagentStop` | A sub-agent spawns or completes |\n| `stop` | The agent loop ends |\n\n## How does the hook collector script work?\n\nThe full script is about 280 lines of bash with no dependencies, available in the [elastic/elasticsearch-labs repository](https://github.com/elastic/elasticsearch-labs/tree/main/supporting-blog-content/security-labs/ai-hooks-collector-scripts) along with a PowerShell port for Windows (which will be shared at a later stage). It reads one JSON payload from stdin, extracts the fields we care about, and appends one line to a JSONL formatted date-rotated log file. Three design decisions shaped it.\n\n**Answer blocking hooks first.** A sensor-only collector still registers the blocking events, because they carry the richest telemetry: `beforeShellExecution` captures the command before it runs, and \\``beforeReadFil`e\\` is the only read event Cursor offers. Registering them means Cursor pauses those actions and waits for a verdict, whether or not you ever intend to say no. The script therefore answers before it does anything else. If it crashed after the response was sent, nothing would hang; if it crashed before, the agent could stall on filesystem errors that have nothing to do with the user. So the very first thing the script does after reading stdin is approve (trimmed here to the Cursor events):\n\n```shell\n# Respond to blocking hooks before any filesystem work, so a\n# logging failure can never hold up the agent.\nif [[ \"$INPUT\" == *'\"hook_event_name\"'*'\"beforeMCPExecution\"'* ]] || \\\n   [[ \"$INPUT\" == *'\"hook_event_name\"'*'\"beforeShellExecution\"'* ]] || \\\n   [[ \"$INPUT\" == *'\"hook_event_name\"'*'\"beforeReadFile\"'* ]] || \\\n   [[ \"$INPUT\" == *'\"hook_event_name\"'*'\"subagentStart\"'* ]]; then\n  echo '{\"permission\":\"allow\"}'\nfi\n```\n\nIn Cursor's response schema, `allow` proceeds, `deny` blocks, and `ask` forces a confirmation prompt. Cursor also fails open by default: if the hook process dies without responding, the action proceeds, and a hook that should block on failure can opt into `failClosed: true` instead. The combination means the worst case of a collector bug is a missing log line rather than a blocked engineer.\n\n**Detect which surface fired the hook.** By surface we mean the client the agent ran in: the IDE, the CLI, or a remote session. A single log stream stays useful only if you can tell them apart, and the environment gives it away: the Cursor IDE is a VS Code fork, so hook processes it spawns inherit VS Code environment variables, while the CLI sets none of them (trimmed to the Cursor branch; the full script uses the same technique to tag other agents' surfaces)\n\n```shell\nif [[ \"$INPUT\" == *'\"cursor_version\"'* ]] || [ -n \"${CURSOR_VERSION:-}\" ]; then\n  AGENT=\"cursor\"\n  if [ \"${CURSOR_CODE_REMOTE:-}\" = \"true\" ]; then\n    IDE=\"remote\"\n  elif [ -n \"${VSCODE_PID:-}\" ] || [ -n \"${VSCODE_CWD:-}\" ] || [ -n \"${VSCODE_IPC_HOOK:-}\" ]; then\n    IDE=\"cursor\"      # the IDE is a VS Code fork; its env vars leak through\n  else\n    IDE=\"cursor-cli\"  # the CLI sets none of them\n  fi\nfi\n```\n\n**Promote the fields you will query.** Each log entry carries the original hook payload under a `raw` key, plus identity (`user`, `email`, `host`) and a set of top-level fields extracted from the payload: `hook_event_name`, `tool_name`, `command`, `file_path`, `mcp_server`, `model`, `session_id`, and duration. A finished entry looks like this:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"timestamp\": \"2026-06-02T09:14:31Z\",\n  \"user\": \"adeveloper\",\n  \"email\": \"adeveloper@example.com\",\n  \"host\": \"macbook-dev42\",\n  \"agent\": \"cursor\",\n  \"ide\": \"cursor-cli\",\n  \"model\": \"some-model-id\",\n  \"session_id\": \"f3b9...\",\n  \"hook_event_name\": \"beforeShellExecution\",\n  \"tool_name\": \"Shell\",\n  \"command\": \"npm test -- --watch=false\",\n  \"file_path\": null,\n  \"mcp_server\": null,\n  \"final_status\": null,\n  \"duration\": null,\n  \"duration_ms\": null,\n  \"event\": { \"kind\": \"event\", \"category\": \"process\", \"type\": \"start\",\n             \"action\": \"beforeShellExecution\", \"outcome\": null, \"duration\": null },\n  \"raw\": { \"...\": \"original hook payload, abridged\" }\n}\n```\n\nThe `event` object follows [Elastic Common Schema (ECS)](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/ecs) conventions (`event.category`, `event.type`, `event.outcome`), which makes the data line up with the rest of your security indices for correlation. We did not start with these promoted fields, and the section on shipping explains why we added them.\n\nThe script keeps logs readable only by the owner (`chmod 0600`), rotates by date, and opportunistically deletes files older than 30 days. Local retention is short on purpose; Elasticsearch is the system of record.\n\n## Configuring Cursor hooks\n\nCursor reads a [`hooks.json`](https://cursor.com/docs/agent/hooks) that maps each event to a command. Deployed system-wide on macOS, it lives at `/Library/Application Support/Cursor/hooks.json`, and both the IDE and the CLI pick it up, so one file covers both surfaces:\n\n```json\n{\n  \"version\": 1,\n  \"hooks\": {\n    \"sessionStart\":          [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"beforeShellExecution\":  [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"afterShellExecution\":   [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"beforeMCPExecution\":    [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"afterMCPExecution\":     [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"postToolUse\":           [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"postToolUseFailure\":    [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"afterFileEdit\":         [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"beforeReadFile\":        [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"subagentStart\":         [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"subagentStop\":          [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"sessionEnd\":            [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }],\n    \"stop\":                  [{ \"command\": \"/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh\" }]\n  }\n}\n```\n\nTwo gotchas cost us real time. The first: the script must not live under `/Library/Application Support/`. Cursor splits hook command paths on spaces, so a script under a path containing a space silently never runs. We keep the script at `/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/` and only the JSON config under the Cursor directory.\n\nThe second: Cursor reads `hooks.json` only at startup, so the hooks stay dormant on every machine until Cursor restarts. Our deployment showed green everywhere while the pipeline stayed silent, and the fix was operational rather than technical: the MDM deployment tooling now detects a running Cursor and prompts the user to restart it. Budget for that restart in your rollout plan, because until it happens you are deployed but not collecting.\n\nCursor also ships a headless CLI agent (cursor-agent), the same one used in CI jobs or scripted runs, and it fires the same hooks as the IDE. Confirm that path is covered too:\n\n```shell\ncursor-agent --print \"say hello\"\ntail -1 ~/.config/ai-hooks/logs/tool-calls-$(date -u +%F).jsonl\n```\n\nThe output shows `\"agent\":\"cursor\",\"ide\":\"cursor-cli\"` instead of ide=cursor, confirming the CLI path is tagged and captured separately from the IDE.\n\n## Deploying Cursor hooks without MDM\n\nNot every machine sits under device management. Our macOS and Windows fleets received the script and hooks file through their management tooling, but our Linux workstations have no equivalent channel. What they do have is Cursor itself, enrolled in our enterprise tenant, and Cursor's console can push a hook configuration to every enrolled client through [Cloud Distribution](https://cursor.com/docs/agent/hooks#cloud-distribution-enterprise-only). The console distributes a command rather than files, so a `hooks.json` that points at `/usr/local/share/ai-hooks/log-tool-calls.sh` is useless if nothing ever placed that script on the box.\n\nWe solved this by making the hook command carry its own payload. The command holds the collector script gzipped and base64-encoded, pinned to a SHA-256 hash. On each invocation, it checks whether the installed script matches the hash, and if it is missing or stale, it decodes the payload, verifies the hash again before writing, installs the script to the user's Cursor directory, and then executes it. Every failure path exits zero, so a decode or write problem degrades to a missing log line rather than a broken agent, which is the same fail-open contract as the rest of the pipeline. A machine with no prior collector self-installs the first time an agent fires a hook, and a version bump is one hash change in the console. The full command is in the repository next to the collector.\n\n## Sending hook logs to Elasticsearch with Elastic Agent\n\nAs InfoSec is Customer Zero at Elastic, the Elastic Agent is already rolled out to every endpoint, so collection was one new integration policy: a [Custom Logs (filestream)](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/filestream) input pointed at the hook log glob, with a `decode_json_fields` processor to parse each line. Configuring the integration was done quickly; getting the log format right took much longer.\n\n```\n# Custom Logs (filestream) integration settings\npaths:\n  - /Users/*/.config/ai-hooks/logs/tool-calls-*.jsonl   # macOS\n  - /home/*/.config/ai-hooks/logs/tool-calls-*.jsonl    # Linux\n  - C:\\Users\\*\\.config\\ai-hooks\\logs\\tool-calls-*.jsonl # Windows\ndata_stream.dataset: ai_hooks\nprocessors:\n  - decode_json_fields:\n      fields: [\"message\"]\n      target: \"ai_hooks\"\n      add_error_key: true\n```\n\nEach line of the JSONL log file becomes one event in a `logs-ai_hooks-*` data stream, with every field from that line under the `ai_hooks.*` prefix.\n\nHere is the lesson that reshaped the log format. Our first version logged only identity plus the raw payload, on the theory that `decode_json_fields` would expand everything and Kibana would sort it out. That theory was technically true: the data was all there, nested under `ai_hooks.raw.*`, three levels deep, with payload shapes that varied by hook type. Building a dashboard on `ai_hooks.raw.tool_input.command` for one event type and `ai_hooks.raw.command` for another was miserable, and our nested format turned Discover sessions into archaeology.\n\nWe extended the script to promote the queryable fields (`tool_name`, `command`, `file_path`, `mcp_server`) to the top level, and every downstream artifact got simpler. If you adopt one thing from this post beyond the script itself, make it this: structure your log line for the queries you want to run, and keep `raw` as the escape hatch rather than the interface.\n\n## Hunting across agent activity with ES|QL\n\nWith promoted fields, the questions that motivated the project become ES|QL one-liners. Which tools do agents call most across the fleet:\n\n```\nFROM logs-ai_hooks-*\n| WHERE ai_hooks.tool_name IS NOT NULL\n| STATS calls = COUNT(*) BY ai_hooks.tool_name\n| SORT calls DESC\n| LIMIT 10\n```\n\nOn our fleet, file reads dominate by roughly four to one over shell execution, which matched nobody's intuition: picture what a coding agent does and you picture it running commands, so shell felt like the obvious leader. Most of what an agent actually does is reconnaissance of your own codebase, reading before acting.\n\nEvery shell command an agent ran on a given host, newest first:\n\n```\nFROM logs-ai_hooks-*\n| WHERE ai_hooks.hook_event_name == \"beforeShellExecution\"\n  AND ai_hooks.host == \"macbook-dev42\"\n| KEEP @timestamp, ai_hooks.user, ai_hooks.agent, ai_hooks.command\n| SORT @timestamp DESC\n| LIMIT 50\n```\n\nAgents that touched credential material ([T1552.001, Credentials in Files](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1552/001/)):\n\n```\nFROM logs-ai_hooks-*\n| WHERE ai_hooks.file_path LIKE \"*.env\"\n   OR ai_hooks.file_path LIKE \"*.pem\"\n   OR ai_hooks.file_path RLIKE \".*/credentials(\\\\.[A-Za-z0-9]+)?$\"\n| STATS reads = COUNT(*) BY ai_hooks.user, ai_hooks.host, ai_hooks.file_path\n| SORT reads DESC\n```\n\nExpect this one to be noisy in a good way: agents read `.env` files constantly because that is where connection settings live. The value is the baseline. One tuning note: a bare `*credentials*` wildcard also matches project and plan file names that happen to contain the word, so we anchor the pattern to the filename itself, `credentials` or `credentials.\u003cext\u003e`, to keep the results to actual credential files. Once you know a host normally shows about four such reads a day, twenty reads in an hour against paths outside the working repo is a signal worth a look. One operational note: ES|QL returns at most 1,000 rows unless you raise the `LIMIT``, so treat a result that comes back at exactly 1,000 rows as truncated.\n\nWhich MCP servers are in use, and how widely:\n\n```\nFROM logs-ai_hooks-*\n| WHERE ai_hooks.mcp_server IS NOT NULL\n| STATS calls = COUNT(*), users = COUNT_DISTINCT(ai_hooks.user) BY ai_hooks.mcp_server\n| SORT users DESC\n```\n\nThis query answers a question that has nothing to do with security: who's actually running which MCP server. Before hooks, our list of MCP servers in use was whatever people remembered to mention. After, it was a live table, and the long tail surprised us: more than 300 distinct servers, and 86% of them used by only one or two people.\n\nDownload-and-execute patterns worth reviewing ([T1105, Ingress Tool Transfer](https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1105/)):\n\n```\nFROM logs-ai_hooks-*\n| WHERE ai_hooks.command LIKE \"*curl*\"\n  AND (ai_hooks.command LIKE \"*| sh*\" OR ai_hooks.command LIKE \"*| bash*\")\n| KEEP @timestamp, ai_hooks.user, ai_hooks.host, ai_hooks.command\n| SORT @timestamp DESC\n```\n\nWe run variants of these as saved queries behind two dashboards: an activity overview (events over time by surface, top tools, models in use, active machines) and a security monitor (shell commands, MCP calls, failed tool calls, file edits, per-user activity). Both are standard [Lens](https://www.elastic.co/docs/explore-analyze/visualize/lens) panels over the same data stream; nothing about the visualization layer is exotic, which is the point of normalizing early.\n\n## Hardening and privacy for agent audit logs\n\nOnce this data existed, the decisions that mattered most had little to do with the pipeline.\n\n* **Restrict who can read it.** Hook logs are a detailed record of how individual engineers work, so we treated them like DNS logs: useful in aggregate, sensitive per-person. In Elasticsearch we excluded the `ai_hooks.*` field namespace from general-purpose security roles using [field-level security](https://www.elastic.co/docs/deploy-manage/users-roles/cluster-or-deployment-auth/controlling-access-at-document-field-level), leaving full access to the small team that owns the pipeline:\n\n```json\nPOST /_security/role/secops_general\n{\n  \"indices\": [\n    {\n      \"names\": [\"logs-*\"],\n      \"privileges\": [\"read\"],\n      \"field_security\": {\n        \"grant\": [\"*\"],\n        \"except\": [\"ai_hooks.*\"]\n      }\n    }\n  ]\n}\n```\n\n* **Collect metadata and leave content alone.** The script logs the command line, the file path, and the tool name. It does not log file contents, prompts, or model responses. That line is what made the rollout conversation with engineering straightforward instead of adversarial: we could state plainly that this is security telemetry, on par with process auditing, and that nobody is reading code or conversations.\n\n* **Say all of that out loud.** We published an internal page describing exactly what is collected, who can query it, and why, before the fleet deployment started, and linked it from the rollout announcement. The questions we got afterward were about edge cases, and there was no pushback on the premise.\n\n## What 13 million tool calls reveal about AI coding agent behavior\n\nThe aggregate numbers from the first two months after the May rollout, rounded, came to over 13 million tool-call events from more than 1,100 machines and nearly 900 distinct users. The CLI surface alone accounts for nearly a fifth of the events, which we would never have guessed from install counts. The busiest single event type was `postToolUse`, and `beforeReadFile` came second. That confirmed the read-heavy profile held across the whole fleet, not only on early-adopter machines.\n\nThose numbers arrived fast. The proof-of-concept ran on one laptop for about six weeks while the script grew the field promotion, the CLI detection, and a series of small survival fixes (resolve the home directory from the passwd database when `HOME` is unset; refuse to run without piped stdin so a stray manual invocation cannot hang on `cat`). Then management tooling pushed it to the whole fleet, and within a week the pipeline went from a trickle to nearly a million events.\n\nHere are two operational notes for anyone repeating this. First, fail-open is the correct default and you should still measure it: `postToolUseFailure` events told us when hooks themselves misbehaved after agent updates. Second, agent vendors ship fast and hook payloads change; the `raw` field meant new payload fields were captured from day one even before we promoted them.\n\n## What are the limitations of hook-based AI agent auditing?\n\nThis section exists because a defender will ask all of these questions anyway.\n\nA developer with admin rights can remove the hooks configuration or edit the script, and on macOS the per-user log file is writable by its owner before shipping. This is workforce telemetry under the same trust model as any endpoint agent, and it is tamper-evident at the fleet level (a host whose events stop while the machine stays active is itself a signal) rather than tamper-proof. Pair it with an inventory source you control, such as [osquery](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/osquery), to detect machines where the agent is installed, but no hook events arrive. Elastic has released Shadow AI detection packs with [OSquery Manager v1.3.3](https://www.elastic.co/docs/reference/integrations/osquery_manager) that inventory local LLMs, MCP configurations, and AI browser extensions across the fleet; hooks tell you what agents do, OSquery tells you where they exist. If you want to build something more custom, this blog is here to help you out; a future post will cover OSquery packs and Shadow AI detection in depth.\n\n\nCoverage is also bounded by the hook events the vendor chooses to expose. We see tool calls, and we do not see the prompt or the model's reasoning, so intent stays out of frame. A hostile agent steered through prompt injection would show up here only through its actions. That is still far more than we could see before, and actions are ultimately what an incident responder needs. Cloud Distribution-pushed hooks only reach the enterprise tenant, so a personal Cursor login is invisible to this pipeline.\n\n## Getting started: try it on one laptop\n\nThe collector script, the hooks configuration, the self-installing console command, and the platform installers are in the [supporting repo](https://github.com/elastic/elasticsearch-labs/tree/main/supporting-blog-content/security-labs/ai-hooks-collector-scripts). With Elastic Agent already deployed, the path from zero to first dashboard is short: install the script and the hooks file, then add one custom logs integration. Start on your own laptop, run one agent session, and look at what lands in `logs-ai_hooks-*`. The first time you watch an agent's afternoon of work replay as structured events, you will have a much more concrete opinion about what your fleet's blind spot has been hiding."},"schema_markup":{"faq":{"steps":[{"name_l10n":"How do you audit what AI coding agents do on developer machines?","accepted_answer_l10n":"Cursor exposes hooks that run an external program on every tool call, and other coding agents offer equivalent hooks.
